=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N bags, labeled bag 1, bag 2, \ldots, bag N.
Bag i (1 \leq i \leq N) contains A_i stones.
Takahashi can perform the following operation any number of times, possibly zero:

Choose two bags A and B, and move all stones from bag A into bag B.

Find the number of different possible values for the following after repeating the operation.

- B_1 \oplus B_2 \oplus \cdots \oplus B_N, where B_i is the final number of stones in bag i.
Here, \oplus denotes bitwise XOR.


About bitwise XOR
For non-negative integers a and b, the bitwise XOR a \oplus b is defined as follows:


In the binary representation of a \oplus b, the digit in the 2^k place (k \ge 0) is 1 if and only if exactly one of the digits in the 2^k place of a and b is 1; otherwise, it is 0.


For example, 3 \oplus 5 = 6 (in binary, 011 \oplus 101 = 110).
In general, for k non-negative integers x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_k, their bitwise XOR x_1 \oplus x_2 \oplus \cdots \oplus x_k is defined as (\cdots((x_1 \oplus x_2) \oplus x_3) \oplus \cdots) \oplus x_k, which does not depend on the order of x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_k.

It can be proved that under the constraints of this problem, the number of possible values is finite.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the number of different possible values for B_1 \oplus B_2 \oplus \cdots \oplus B_N after repeating the operation.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 12
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^{17}
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
2 5 7

Sample Output 1

3

For example, if Takahashi chooses bags 1 and 3 for the operation, then the numbers of stones in bags 1, 2, 3 become 0, 5, 9.
If he stops at this point, the XOR is 0 \oplus 5 \oplus 9 = 12.
The other possible XOR values after repeating the operation are 0 and 14.
Therefore, the possible values are 0, 12, 14; there are three values, so the output is 3.

Sample Input 2

2
100000000000000000 100000000000000000

Sample Output 2

2

Sample Input 3

6
71 74 45 34 31 60

Sample Output 3

84

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's design recursive enumeration carefully.

Option 1: Standard recursion over index i, list sums, current xor.

Pseudo:

def dfs(i, x, sums):
    if i == N:
        ans.add(x)
        return
    a = A[i]
    # assign to existing groups
    for j in range(len(sums)):
        s = sums[j]
        sums[j] = s + a
        dfs(i+1, x ^ s ^ (s + a), sums)
        sums[j] = s
    # start new group
    sums.append(a)
    dfs(i+1, x ^ a, sums)
    sums.pop()

But careful: If we start new group, x changes by XOR a (since adding group sum a). If assign to existing group, old sum s removed, new sum s+a added: x' = x xor s xor (s+a). This is correct.

At i=0, sums empty, x=0. Start new group only. Then process.

def dfs(i, x):
    if i == n:
        ans.add(x); return
    a = A[i]
    for j, s in enumerate(sums):
        ns = s + a
        sums[j] = ns

Let's write code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    ans = set()
    sums = []
    def dfs(i, x):
        if i == N:
            ans.add(x)
            return
        a = A[i]
        # existing groups
        for idx in range(len(sums)):
            s = sums[idx]
            ns = s + a
            sums[idx] = ns
            dfs(i + 1, x ^ s ^ ns)
            sums[idx] = s
        # new group
        sums.append(a)
        dfs(i + 1, x ^ a)
        sums.pop()
    dfs(0, 0)
    print(len(ans))
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Our `sums` list stores only sums, not member sets. Does this cause a problem? Important!

Let's examine. We process elements in order. We maintain only sums of groups, not which original elements are in each group. If two groups have the same sum, they are distinct groups in the partition (different subsets of indices). Assigning the current element to group 0 vs group 1 yields different set partitions, but our state `sums` after assignment might be identical? These are different orderings. Since we iterate over indices, we will generate both. Good. But if we had used a set of sums, would lose. We use list, so distinct group identities by position. However, do we ever have two states with same sums list but different actual partitions? Since we process elements in order and maintain list of sums in order of group creation. The list order encodes group identities: group 0 is the first group created, group 1 second, etc. But is that enough to uniquely determine the partition? Yes, because when a new group is created, it's appended; when an element joins an existing group, it joins the group at that position. Thus the sequence of choices (which position or new) is a restricted growth string? Actually assigning to existing groups by index of creation yields a canonical representation of set partitions (restricted growth function): each element's group label is either an existing label or new max label. The labels are ordered by creation. This uniquely represents the partition. So using sums list by label is fine. Even if two sums equal, positions distinguish.

But wait: Our recursion allows assigning to any existing group index, including labels that may not be in increasing order? The labels are creation order. For a set partition, if we process elements in order, the group label of each element is the index (creation order) of its block. This is exactly restricted growth string: first element label 0; each new element can choose any label from 0..max_label or max_label+1. This generates each set partition exactly once. Our `sums` list length = max_label+1, and sums by label. Good.

DP over subsets with sets:
f = [set() for _ in range(1<<N)]
f[0] = {0}
for mask in range(1,1<<N):
    lb = mask & -mask
    rest = mask ^ lb
    sub = rest
    while True:
        part = sub | lb
        s = sum_A[part]
        for v in f[mask ^ part]:
            f[mask].add(s ^ v)
        if sub == 0: break
        sub = (sub - 1) & rest
answer len(f[full])

Maybe use fixed-size list `sums = [0]*N` and `cnt` variable. Then for j in range(cnt): s=sums[j]; ...; after loop sums[cnt]=a; dfs(i+1, x^a, cnt+1). This avoids append/pop and maybe faster. Let's consider.

Recursive function signature `dfs(i, x, cnt)` where cnt = number of groups. `sums` is fixed list length N. At depth i, cnt <= i. For existing groups j in range(cnt): update sums[j], recurse with same cnt. Then restore. Then start new group: sums[cnt] = a; recurse with cnt+1. No need to clear because overwritten later. This is efficient.

Code:

sums = [0]*N
def dfs(i, x, cnt):
    if i == N:
        ans.add(x)
        return
    a = A[i]
    for j in range(cnt):
        s = sums[j]
        ns = s + a
        sums[j] = ns
        dfs(i+1, x ^ s ^ ns, cnt)
        sums[j] = s
    sums[cnt] = a
    dfs(i+1, x ^ a, cnt+1)

This is better. At i=0, cnt=0, only new group. Good.

Now, note: When cnt groups exist, labels 0..cnt-1. Starting new group at index cnt. Since we process elements in order, this is RGS. Good.

Code:

def dfs(i, x, cnt, A=A, N=N, sums=sums, ans_add=ans.add):
    if i == N:
        ans_add(x)
        return
    a = A[i]
    for j in range(cnt):
        s = sums[j]
        ns = s + a
        sums[j] = ns
        dfs(i + 1, x ^ s ^ ns, cnt)
        sums[j] = s
    sums[cnt] = a
    dfs(i + 1, x ^ a, cnt + 1)

Now, let's maybe write a proof outline for myself:
- Operation merges bags; final state corresponds to a partition of original bags; final XOR is XOR of sums of blocks.
- Recursively construct every partition by processing bags in order: for each bag, either put it into one of the existing blocks or create a new block. This is bijective.
- Maintain block sums and current XOR. Adding a to a block with sum s changes XOR from x to x xor s xor (s+a). Creating new block changes x to x xor a.
- Insert leaf XOR into set. Output set size.

Now, let's think about if we need to handle N=0? Constraints N>=2. Good.

Let's maybe write final code:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:1+N]
    ans = set()
    sums = [0] * N

    def dfs(i, x, cnt, A=A, N=N, sums=sums, ans_add=ans.add):
        if i == N:
            ans_add(x)
            return
        a = A[i]
        for j in range(cnt):
            s = sums[j]
            ns = s + a
            sums[j] = ns
            dfs(i + 1, x ^ s ^ ns, cnt)
            sums[j] = s
        sums[cnt] = a
        dfs(i + 1, x ^ a, cnt + 1)

    dfs(0, 0, 0)
    print(len(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

But let's not finalize yet; let's explore if there is a hidden twist: The operation can be performed "any number of times, possibly zero". It says "Choose two bags A and B, and move all stones from bag A into bag B." If bag A and B are chosen, and A has stones, B gets A+B. If B is empty, it's just moving. This operation can also be used to "swap" contents? Suppose bag1 has x, bag2 has y. Move bag1 to bag2: bag1=0, bag2=x+y. Move bag2 (which now has x+y) to bag1: bag1=x+y, bag2=0. So contents can be moved to any bag, but cannot split. So final groups are connected components of a directed merge forest. Any partition can be achieved by, for each group, choosing one bag as target and moving all other bags in the group into it. Yes.

Let's see if we can optimize further using iterative RGS enumeration with incremental XOR and group sums, avoiding function calls. We can simulate the recursive DFS using an explicit stack of "actions" to avoid Python function call overhead? But manual stack operations in Python may also be overhead. However, we can implement a loop that generates RGS in lexicographic order and updates sums/x incrementally? Let's explore.

Standard RGS enumeration:
Initialize `a = [0]*N` (labels), `max_label = 0`.
At each step, we have current RGS for first i elements? To enumerate all RGS, we can use algorithm:
- Start with all labels 0? Actually RGS length N.
- The first RGS is [0,0,...,0] (all in one block). Last is [0,1,2,...,N-1] (all singletons).
- We can generate next RGS in O(N) using algorithm. But computing XOR from scratch O(N) per RGS -> 50M operations. We can update incrementally if we know changes? Complex.

Alternatively, iterative DFS with explicit stack of frames:
We need to perform same operations as recursion but without function calls. We can use a stack of frames (i, x, cnt, next_j, a?). But we also need to restore sums. Let's design.

We can use a stack of "commands" or frames. At each frame, we process one element. The state includes i, x, cnt, and a loop index j. We need to know whether we are in the middle of existing group loop or after loop. We can implement:

stack = [(0, 0, 0, 0, 0)]? Let's define frame: (i, x, cnt, j) where j is next group index to try. But when we start a new group, we need to know if we've finished existing loop. We can use a sentinel j = -1 for new group? Let's think.

Recursive pseudocode:
def dfs(i,x,cnt):
    if i==N: add
    else:
        a=A[i]
        for j in 0..cnt-1:
            s=sums[j]; sums[j]=s+a; dfs(i+1, x^s^(s+a), cnt); sums[j]=s
        sums[cnt]=a; dfs(i+1, x^a, cnt+1)

We can simulate with stack of frames where each frame has state (i, x, cnt, j). At entry, j=0. We process:
- If i==N: add and pop.
- Else if j < cnt: perform transition for existing group j, push current frame with j+1 (so after return continue), then push child frame for i+1 with updated sums/x/cnt. Need restore after child returns. We can restore before pushing? Let's design.

Option: When we process a transition, we modify sums[j], push a frame representing "after child returns, restore sums[j]" and then push child. But easier: Use frame with `phase`.

Let's implement with stack of tuples `(i, x, cnt, j, s_old?)`? Hmm.

We can use a stack of "continuations" that include restoration info. For each recursive call, we need to restore after it returns. We can push a "restore" action onto stack before pushing child. Example:

stack = [(0, 0, 0, 0, 0)]? Let's define frames as `(i, x, cnt, j)` where j is current group index to try. At top:
- If i == N: add, pop.
- If j < cnt:
    a = A[i]
    s = sums[j]
    ns = s + a
    sums[j] = ns
    # We need to continue this frame at j+1 after child returns.
    # Push continuation frame with j+1 and same i,x,cnt? But x for continuation should be original x (before transition), not child x. The continuation will try next j with original x. So push (i, x, cnt, j+1).
    # Push child frame (i+1, x_child, cnt, 0).
    stack.append((i, x, cnt, j+1))
    stack.append((i+1, x_child, cnt, 0))
    # But we also need to restore sums[j] after child returns. The continuation frame should restore before processing next j. We can restore when we pop the continuation frame. How to know? The continuation frame can carry the old s and j to restore. So frame: (i, x, cnt, j+1, restore_j, restore_s) maybe.
- If j == cnt:
    # start new group
    a = A[i]
    sums[cnt] = a
    # push continuation? No more after new group, just child.
    stack.append((i+1, x ^ a, cnt+1, 0))
    # Need restore? No, because after child returns, this frame is done. But sums[cnt] may be overwritten by sibling? Actually after returning to parent, parent will restore its own sums. We don't need to clear sums[cnt] as argued. But in iterative stack, when child returns, we pop and continue with next frame. The parent continuation frame (if any) will restore its own old sum. The stale sums[cnt] is harmless. So no restore needed.

But wait, if we push continuation frame with restore info, we need to restore sums[j] before continuing. We can store `old_s` in the continuation frame and restore when it becomes top. However, if we push continuation frame below child, after child pops, continuation frame is top. We can have it restore immediately upon processing. But the continuation frame also needs to know it has already restored. We can design frame as `(i, x, cnt, j, old_s)` where j is the next index to try, and old_s is the sum to restore for index j-1? Let's formalize.

Frame types:
- A "work" frame for state (i, x, cnt, j) means we are at dfs(i,x,cnt) and about to try group j (0 <= j <= cnt). If j < cnt, we perform transition for group j, then push a frame to continue with j+1 after restoring, and push child.
- If j == cnt, we perform new group transition and push child.

But after child returns, we need to restore the sum we changed before continuing. We can store the old sum in the continuation frame. Let's define continuation frame as `(i, x, cnt, j_next, old_s)` where j_next = j+1, and old_s is the sum that was changed at index j. When this frame is popped, we first restore `sums[j_next-1] = old_s`, then process as a work frame with j = j_next. But how to distinguish continuation from work? We can include a flag or use `old_s is not None`. Simpler: use a frame tuple `(i, x, cnt, j, old_s, is_continuation)`.

Alternatively, we can restore before pushing child? No, child needs modified sums. So restore after child.

Let's implement iterative stack with frames as lists to mutate? Tuples okay.

Pseudo:

stack = [(0, 0, 0, 0, None, False)]  # i,x,cnt,j,old_s,cont?
while stack:
    frame = stack.pop()
    i, x, cnt, j, old_s, cont = frame
    if cont:
        sums[j-1] = old_s
        # now continue with same i,x,cnt,j (not j-1)
        # We can just set cont=False and process.
        # But need avoid recursion; we can loop.
    if i == N:
        ans_add(x)
        continue
    a = A[i]
    if j < cnt:
        s = sums[j]
        ns = s + a
        sums[j] = ns
        # push continuation to restore and try j+1
        stack.append((i, x, cnt, j+1, s, True))
        # push child
        stack.append((i+1, x ^ s ^ ns, cnt, 0, None, False))
    else: # j == cnt
        sums[cnt] = a
        stack.append((i+1, x ^ a, cnt+1, 0, None, False))

But wait: When we pop a continuation frame, we restore sums[j-1] = old_s, then we need to process the state (i,x,cnt,j) as a work frame. The code above after restoring would then go to `if i == N`? But i is not N (since continuation only from non-leaf). Then it would process a=A[i] and if j < cnt, etc. That's correct. But note: The continuation frame's `x` is the original x before the previous transition, not the child's x. Good.

However, there is a problem: When we push continuation frame and child, the stack LIFO pops child first, processes entire subtree, then pops continuation. Good.

But what about the new group transition? We don't push a continuation because after child returns, the current frame is done. But if we simply push child, after child returns, the next frame on stack is the parent's continuation (or another). There is no need to restore sums[cnt]. Good.

Let's test iterative with N=2 A=[1,3].
Initial stack [(0,0,0,0,None,False)]
Pop work i=0,cnt=0,j=0,j==cnt -> new group: sums[0]=1, push child (1,1,1,0,None,False)
Pop child i=1,cnt=1,j=0,j<cnt:
 s=sums[0]=1, ns=4, sums[0]=4
 push continuation (1,1,1,1,1,True)  # i=1,x=1,cnt=1,j_next=1,old_s=1
 push child (2, x^1^4 = 1^1^4=4, cnt=1,0,None,False)
Pop child i=2==N -> ans_add(4)
Pop continuation: restore sums[0]=1; process i=1,x=1,cnt=1,j=1 (j==cnt) -> new group: a=3, sums[1]=3, push child (2, x^3=1^3=2, cnt=2,0,None,False)
Pop child i=2 -> ans_add(2)
Done. Values {4,2}. Good.

But wait, in the continuation frame, after restoring, it processes new group transition. It sets sums[cnt] = a where cnt=1, index1. Good. No restore needed.

Now, does this iterative stack correctly handle multiple existing groups? Let's test N=3 with state where cnt=2. At work frame j=0, push continuation j=1, child. After child, continuation restores sums[0], processes j=1. If j=1 < cnt, push continuation j=2, child. After child, continuation restores sums[1], processes j=2 == cnt, new group. Good.

Now, let's maybe explore if there is a mathematical way to compute answer using "subset sum XOR linear basis" that could be more efficient and memory-light. Let's investigate for a bit, in case recursion might be considered too naive but accepted.

We want set of XORs of block sums over partitions. This is equivalent to evaluating a "set partition" under a function. There is a known concept: "Bell number" enumeration is intended for N<=12. AtCoder constraints often allow O(Bell(N)) or O(3^N). Bell(12)=4.2M, so yes.

Could there be an O(3^N) DP that counts distinct XORs using sets and is more memory efficient? Let's compare. O(3^N) for N=12 = 531k, actually 3^12=531,441, less than Bell. Wait, 3^12 is 531k, much less than Bell(12)=4.2M. A DP over subsets with O(3^N) transitions might be faster! Let's explore.

The recurrence F(S) = { sum(S) } ∪ { a xor b | T proper nonempty subset of S, a∈F(T), b∈F(S\T) }. If we compute F for all subsets, transitions over all T subset S: total 3^N = 531,441. For each transition, we iterate over pairs (a,b) from F(T) and F(S\T). The number of pairs could be large. But maybe we can store F as set and combine. Total operations might be sum over T of |F(T)|*|F(S\T)|, which could be huge (Bell^2). Not good.

But we can compute F(S) using the min-element recurrence with submasks: choose first block containing min element. That recurrence:
F(S) = { sum(T) xor v | T ⊆ S, min(S) ∈ T, v ∈ F(S\T) } with base F(∅)={0}.
This is exactly our recursive enumeration but memoized over subsets. Number of transitions over submasks = 3^N/2? Actually sum over S of 2^{|S|-1} = (3^N-1)/2 = 265,720. For each transition, iterate over v in F(S\T). Total operations equals number of partitions? Let's analyze. For each mask S, each partition of S is counted once? The recurrence chooses the block containing min element, then recursively partitions rest. So total operations across all masks equals number of partitions of all subsets = Bell(N+1)? Wait, for each mask S, the number of leaves in its DP is Bell(|S|). The recurrence transitions count over submasks and v values. The total number of set additions across all masks equals total number of partitions of all masks = Bell(N+1) = 27.6M. That's larger than recursive enumeration's 5M transitions? Wait recursive enumeration total calls 5M, but DP total set additions 27.6M. So recursive enumeration is better.

But DP over subsets stores F for all masks, memory huge. Not good.

Could use recursive memo with min-element recurrence and only store F for masks needed? It would compute F(full) and recursively F(submasks). Number of states 2^N=4096. But F sets can be large. Total set elements across all masks maybe 27.6M. Memory huge. Not good.

Our RGS recursion enumerates each full partition once, 4.2M leaves, and total calls 5M. Much better.

 dfs(1,1,1): a=2, cnt=1, j=0 s=1 ns=3 sums[0]=3 dfs(2,1^1^3=3,1)
  leaf add 3
  restore sums[0]=1
  after loop, sums[1]=2, dfs(2,1^2=3,2)
  leaf add 3
 ans {3} count1. Correct.

N=2 A=[1,3]:
dfs(0): sums[0]=1, dfs(1,1,1)
 j=0 s=1 ns=4 sums[0]=4 dfs(2,1^1^4=4,1) add4 restore
 new sums[1]=3 dfs(2,1^3=2,2) add2
 ans {4,2} count2. Correct.

Let's try to optimize recursion further:
- Use iterative RGS enumeration with incremental sums and XOR maybe can be faster because no function calls. Let's design a clean iterative DFS with explicit stack and test mentally.

We can simulate recursion using a stack of frames where each frame is a tuple `(i, x, cnt, j, old_s)` and a flag. But tuple allocation per transition might be similar to function call. However, we can use a single stack list and append/pop tuples; tuple allocation is cheaper than function call? Maybe.

Let's design a more efficient iterative algorithm using a stack of "actions" as integers and arrays.

We can use a stack `st` of frames, where each frame is a list `[i, x, cnt, j, old_s, phase]`. But list allocation also.

Maybe we can use `itertools` to generate RGS and compute XOR incrementally using "odometer" with backtracking. Let's explore.

We can enumerate RGS in lexicographic order and maintain group sums and current XOR. There is a known algorithm to generate all restricted growth strings in O(1) amortized per string, and we can update group sums/xor incrementally by changing one label at a time. This could be faster and avoid recursion. Let's think.

RGS a[0..N-1] with a[0]=0, a[i] <= 1 + max(a[0..i-1]). We can generate next RGS by finding rightmost position that can be incremented, then reset suffix. When we change labels, we need update group sums: remove A[pos] from old group sum, add to new group sum; update XOR. This is possible. Number of RGS = Bell(12)=4.2M. The total number of label changes across all RGS in lexicographic order? Could be O(Bell(N) * N) maybe, but amortized maybe O(Bell(N)). Let's analyze.

Standard RGS generation:
Start with a = [0]*N (all zeros). At each step, process current RGS. To get next:
- Find largest i such that a[i] < max(a[0..i-1]) + 1? Actually RGS condition: a[i] can be incremented if a[i] < max_so_far + 1. Choose rightmost such i. Then a[i] += 1; for k>i, a[k]=0.
- Update max_so_far for positions.

When we increment a[i] from old to new, we update group sums: group old loses A[i], group new gains A[i]. Then reset suffix to 0: for each k>i, if a[k] != 0, remove A[k] from its old group and add to group 0. This could be O(N) per RGS in worst case. But total changes maybe O(Bell(N)*N)? Let's estimate. For N=12, 4.2M * 12 = 50M updates, maybe okay but more than recursion 5M. Recursion better.

But maybe we can generate RGS in a different order (e.g., recursive) to update incrementally with O(1) per transition, which is our recursion. So recursion is optimal.

Could implement recursion as a generator? No.

Maybe use "set partition enumeration via restricted growth strings" with backtracking using a while loop and arrays, similar to recursion but no function calls. Let's design carefully; it might be faster.

We can maintain:
- `i`: current depth (0..N)
- `x`: current XOR
- `cnt`: number of groups
- `sums`: list of group sums
- `choice`: an array of length N storing the choice made at each depth? For backtracking, we need to know which group was chosen at each level to restore.

Recursive backtracking can be simulated with a stack of choices. At each step, we choose a group for element i. We need to explore all choices. We can maintain `j` array where `j[i]` is the next group index to try for element i. When we go deeper, we store the current choice. This is like DFS.

Algorithm:
Initialize i=0, x=0, cnt=0, sums empty.
At each iteration:
- If i == N: add x, then backtrack.
- Else:
  - If we are at a new node (no current choice), set j[i] = 0.
  - If j[i] <= cnt: (try group j[i])
      If j[i] < cnt: existing group s=sums[j[i]], ns=s+A[i], update sums[j[i]]=ns, x = x^s^ns, push choice (i, j[i], old_s=s, cnt), i += 1.
      If j[i] == cnt: new group, sums[cnt]=A[i], x = x^A[i], push choice (i, j[i], old_s=None, cnt), cnt += 1, i += 1.
      Increment j[i]? Need after returning from child, we need to restore and try next j. We can store j[i] as the choice made, and when backtracking, increment it.
  - Else (j[i] > cnt): no more choices, backtrack.

Let's design with arrays `choice_i`, `choice_j`, `old_s`, `old_cnt`, `old_x`? Actually we can store enough to restore.

Simpler: Use stack of frames as before.

Let's implement iterative with explicit stack of frames using lists of ints for speed.

Frame fields:
- `i`: depth
- `x`: XOR at entry to frame
- `cnt`: number of groups at entry
- `j`: next group index to try (0..cnt)
- `old_s`: if this frame is a continuation after a child, the old sum to restore for the previous choice; else -1.
- `phase`: 0 = new frame, 1 = continuation after existing-group child (need restore), 2 = continuation after new-group child? For new group no restore needed, but we need to know not to process? Actually after new group child returns, the frame is done, so no continuation needed. We can simply not push continuation for new group. For existing group, push continuation.

Let's implement stack of tuples `(i, x, cnt, j, old_s, phase)` where phase=1 means restore `sums[j-1] = old_s` before processing with same i,x,cnt,j. phase=0 means normal.

Pseudo:

stack = [(0, 0, 0, 0, -1, 0)]
while stack:
    i, x, cnt, j, old_s, phase = stack.pop()
    if phase:
        sums[j - 1] = old_s
        # fall through to process normal frame with same i,x,cnt,j
    if i == N:
        ans_add(x)
        continue
    a = A[i]
    if j < cnt:
        s = sums[j]
        ns = s + a
        sums[j] = ns
        # continuation to restore and try j+1
        stack.append((i, x, cnt, j + 1, s, 1))
        # child
        stack.append((i + 1, x ^ s ^ ns, cnt, 0, -1, 0))
    else:  # j == cnt
        sums[cnt] = a
        # no continuation needed
        stack.append((i + 1, x ^ a, cnt + 1, 0, -1, 0))

But wait: For a normal frame with j == cnt, after pushing child, we don't push a continuation. When child returns, the next frame on stack is the parent's continuation (if any). Good.

However, consider a normal frame that was reached as a child from a parent. It has j=0. It will process existing groups and push continuations. Good.

Now, let's test with N=2 A=[1,3] using this iterative stack:
stack [(0,0,0,0,-1,0)]
pop normal i=0,j=0==cnt: sums[0]=1, push child (1,1,1,0,-1,0)
pop child i=1,j=0<cnt: s=1 ns=4 sums[0]=4, push cont (1,1,1,1,1,1), push child (2,4,1,0,-1,0)
pop child leaf add4
pop cont phase=1: restore sums[0]=1; i=1,x=1,cnt=1,j=1; i!=N, a=3, j==cnt: sums[1]=3, push child (2,2,2,0,-1,0)
pop child leaf add2
stack empty. Good.

But wait, after processing existing group j=0, we push continuation with x original, cnt original, j+1. After child returns, continuation restores and processes j=1. Good.

Now, what about a frame with multiple existing groups: At i=1,cnt=2, j=0. Push cont j=1, child. After child, cont restores sums[0], processes j=1. If j=1<cnt, push cont j=2, child. After child, cont restores sums[1], processes j=2==cnt, new group. Good.

Now, what about the new group child: It may modify sums[cnt] and higher. After it returns, no restore. But is it safe if the parent continuation later accesses sums[cnt]? Suppose parent frame had cnt=2, and after processing existing group j=1, it starts new group at index 2 and pushes child. No continuation for new group, so after child returns, parent frame is done and not on stack. The next frame on stack is the grandparent's continuation, which has cnt maybe 1 or 2. It may access sums indices up to its cnt-1. It won't access index 2 if its cnt <=2. If grandparent's cnt=2, it might access index 1, not 2. So stale sums[2] harmless. Good.

Now, does this iterative stack produce exactly the same number of stack operations as recursion? For each existing group transition, pushes 2 tuples; for each new group transition, pushes 1 tuple. Total transitions 5M; existing group transitions = total transitions - new group transitions. Number of new group transitions = number of internal nodes? Actually each partition of prefix has one new group transition at its node. Number of new group transitions = total internal calls? Let's compute: At each state, there is exactly one new group transition (unless leaf). Number of states at depths 0..N-1 = sum_{i=0}^{N-1} Bell(i) = 1 + 1+2+5+...+678570 = 820,988? Wait sum Bell(0..11) = 820,988? Earlier sum Bell(0..11) = 820,988? Let's recalc: B0..B11 = 1+1+2+5+15+52+203+877+4140+21147+115975+678570 = 820,988? Sum: 1+1=2, +2=4, +5=9, +15=24, +52=76, +203=279, +877=1156, +4140=5296, +21147=26443, +115975=142418, +678570=820988. Yes. So new group transitions = 820,988. Existing group transitions = total transitions - new group = 5,034,584 - 820,988 = 4,213,596. Interesting: existing group transitions = leaves - 1? Leaves = 4,213,597. So stack pushes = 2*4,213,596 + 820,988 = 9,248,180 tuples. That's more than recursion calls (5M) but tuple allocation maybe cheaper than function frames? Not sure. Also stack pop 9.2M. Might be slower.

We can optimize by not pushing continuation for existing group? Could use a frame that remembers j and restores when popped, but we already do. Maybe recursion is better.

Could implement iterative with a single stack of frames where each frame is a list and we mutate, avoiding tuple allocation? Still list allocation. Recursion also allocates frames. Hard.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
I’ll implement a recursive enumeration of all set partitions, maintaining the current XOR of group sums.

```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:1 + N]

    ans = set()
    sums = [0] * N

    def dfs(i, x, cnt, A=A, N=N, sums=sums, ans_add=ans.add):
        if i == N:
            ans_add(x)
            return

        a = A[i]

        # Put bag i into an existing group.
        for j in range(cnt):
            s = sums[j]
            ns = s + a
            sums[j] = ns
            dfs(i + 1, x ^ s ^ ns, cnt)
            sums[j] = s

        # Put bag i into a new group.
        sums[cnt] = a
        dfs(i + 1, x ^ a, cnt + 1)

    dfs(0, 0, 0)
    print(len(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
